Inc., 1992 1 \n    35→\n    36→=== PAGE 4 ===\n    37→IN I - TECHNOLOGY ~ \n    38→HOW DO YOU ACHIEVE HIGH QUALITY SOLDER JOINTS? \n    39→The process of producing high quality solder joints is \n    40→similar to the process of learning how to ride a bicycle. \n    41→If you do not understand the fundamentals, it is virtually \n    42→impossible. However, once you understand and imple- \n    43→ment the basics, the results will follow. The goal of all \n    44→SMT process engineers is to repeatedly produce high \n    45→quality solder joints that have fine grain structure and \n    46→excellent wetting characteristics, as shown in the figure \n    47→below: \n    48→SURFACES MUST BE \n    49→CLEAN AND SOLDERABLE \n    50→II MUST BE \n    51→11 SMALL \n    52→CONTROL VARIABLES \n    53→In order to produce high quality solderjoints, all process \n    54→variables must be in control. The five general process \n    55→variables (l) that need to be in control include the mate- \n    56→rials, machines, manpower, methods, and the environ- \n    57→ment. If the process variables are not in control, there is \n    58→a very good chance that soldering defects will exist. \n    59→Let’s take a closer look at each. \n    60→MATERIALS \n    61→The materials must meet functional specifications so \n    62→that the product will perform to specification. Of equal \n    63→importance is the solderability of the assembly. The \n    64→solder paste/flux system must be compatible with the \n    65→base materials of the parts to be soldered. The solder \n    66→paste and parts should be as fresh as possible to enhance \n    67→solderability. MACHINES \n    68→The machines used in the process must be able to perform \n    69→to their specifications. The machines should operate \n    70→within the range for which they are designed. Pushing a \n    71→machine beyond its operating limits will ultimately cause \n    72→a problem. The machines must be reliable and service- \n    73→able. \n    74→MANPOWER \n    75→The manpower must be properly trained. All machines in \n    76→the line require knowledgeable operators and mainte- \n    77→nance personnel. A machine is only as good as the person \n    78→operating it. \n    79→METHODS \n    80→The methods and procedures must be defined and strictly \n    81→adhered to. Inventory procedures should be implemented \n    82→to ensure that components and boards do not stay on the \n    83→shelf too long. Problems should be fully documented \n    84→including all data that pertains to it. \n    85→ENVIRONMENT \n    86→Finally, the processing environment must be controlled. \n    87→This includes both the environment inside and outside of \n    88→the machines. For example, if the humidity in the solder- \n    89→ing area is too high, this can cause the solder paste to \n    90→absorb moisture, whichmay cause problems duringreflow . \n    91→If the temperature in the room is not controlled, the \n    92→thermal profile in the reflow oven may be affected, which \n    93→again may cause a defect. \n    94→The purpose of this text is to address one aspect of high \n    95→quality SMT soldering, the reflow process. \n    96→Producing high quality solder joints \n    97→requires that five general process \n    98→variables need to be in control; the \n    99→materials, machines, manpower, \n   100→methods, and the environment. \n   101→2 Section I - Technology \n   102→\n   103→=== PAGE 5 ===\n   104→WHAT IS THE IMPORTANCE OF THE PROFILE? \n   105→I I I I I I 1; I :I I I I I In order to fully understand Surface Mount Technology \n   106→(SMT) reflow, one needs to have an understanding of \n   107→what is being accomplished. One can take too simplistic \n   108→a viewpoint in that all you need to do is to heat solder \n   109→to a temperature greater than its melting point. One can \n   110→also take too complicated a viewpoint in believing that a \n   111→certain wavelength of infrared (IR) is required, or that a \n   112→certain percentage of convection is required. Perhaps the \n   113→most astute comment made on surface mount reflow was \n   114→by an individual whom had just completed an evaluation \n   115→of vapor phase, panel IR, and lamp IR equipment nearly \n   116→five years ago. After coming up with virtually identical \n   117→soldering defect results on all three types of soldering \n   118→equipment, the following comment was made: “If you \n   119→could hit the correct reflow profile using buckets of hot \n   120→water, you would accomplish high quality, low defect \n   121→solder joints.” Although the above comment was made \n   122→in jest (hot water is not quite hot enough to reflow solder), \n   123→there is considerable truth to what was said. I \n   124→REFLOW PROFILE REQUIREMENT \n   125→High quality, low defect soldering requires identifying \n   126→the optimum temperature profile for reflowing the solder \n   127→paste, and repeating over and over. Every solder joint on \n   128→every board, needs to be heated similarily if the desired \n   129→soldering results are to be accomplished. It does not \n   130→matter how you apply the heat to the solder joint. What \n   131→does matter is that the heat is applied to the solder joint \n   132→in a controlled manner. The heating and cooling rise \n   133→rates must be compatible with the solder paste and \n   134→components. The amount of time that the assembly is \n   135→exposed to certain temperatures must be defined and \n   136→maintained. In other words, the solder reflow profile \n   137→must first be defined and then maintained. 280- \n   138→260- \n   139→240- \n   140→T 220- \n   141→High quality, low defect soldering \n   142→requires identifying the optimum \n   143→temperature profile for reflowing \n   144→the solder paste, and repeating it \n   145→over and over. I WETTING ; ‘ TIME , \n   146→30-60 .’ : SEC ~ \n   147→TYPICAL PROFILE \n   148→A typical profile consists of a preheat, dryout, and reflow \n   149→section (zone) where different heating rates are used. In \n   150→the preheat section, the goal is to fully preheat the SMT \n   151→assembly to temperatures generally between 100°C to \n   152→150°C. The most critical parameter in the preheat section \n   153→is to minimize the rate of rise to less than 2”C/second. \n   154→The main concern is minimizing thermal shock on the \n   155→components of the assembly. Multilayer ceramic chip \n   156→capacitors can be vulnerable to cracking if heated too \n   157→fast. Solder paste can spatter also, if heated too fast. \n   158→The second heating section, referred to as the dryout, \n   159→soak, or preflow zone, is used primarily to ensure that \n   160→the solder paste is fully dried before hitting reflow \n   161→temperatures. This zone also acts as a flux activation \n   162→zone for some solder pastes. Solder reflow is accom- \n   163→plished in the reflow zone, where the solder paste is \n   164→elevated to a temperature greater than the melting point \n   165→of the solder. For 63/37 Eutectic solder, the melting \n   166→temperature is 183°C. This temperature must be ex- \n   167→ceeded by approximately 20°C to ensure quality reflow. \n   168→The amount of time the solder joint is above the melting \n   169→point is referred to as the “wetting time”. The wetting \n   170→time is normally 30 to 60 seconds for most pastes. If the \n   171→wetting time is excessive, intermetallics may occur in \n   172→the joint, which result in brittle solder joints. In sum- \n   173→mary, define the profile, and hit it. \n   174→Section I - Technology 3 \n   175→\n   176→=== PAGE 6 ===\n   177→HOW MANY HEATING ZONES ARE REQUIRED? \n   178→This question frequently arrives and can be a source of \n   179→confusion. One theory is that if ten zones are good, then \n   180→twenty zones are better. While in theory this may be true, \n   181→in practicality it may not be true. While it improves the \n   182→flexibility of oven, it also adds to the complexity and \n   183→makes oven set-up more difficult. \n   184→Let’s review this question from the standpoint of how \n   185→many zones are really needed. Reflowing solder paste \n   186→typically requires two or three different heating rates to \n   187→accomplish the process. In the figure below, there are \n   188→three “typical” profile bands for RMA, water soluble, \n   189→and no-clean solder pastes. These profiles are rather \n   190→generic, and one should consult with their solder paste \n   191→vendor to determine the desired profile. All of the pastes \n   192→in the figure could be successfully reflowed using \n   193→three zones. reflow is that if you can control your top and bottom \n   194→heaters separately, then double sided reflow can be \n   195→accomplished (without reflowing side A twice). This \n   196→will not work with most SMT reflow processes, as most \n   197→products are too thermally thin. Independent topbottom \n   198→heating control does help in machines with dual convey- \n   199→ors that are located at different heights in the chamber. \n   200→Product warpage may also be minimized through proper \n   201→control. The bottom line is that most machines have this \n   202→feature, but most often the top and bottom heaters will be \n   203→set at the same value. \n   204→Edge heating zones become significant when edge con- \n   205→veyors are used. Edge conveyors act as heat sinks on the \n   206→edge of the product. Two zones of edge heat (one front, \n   207→one back) will assist in compensating for edge con- \n   208→veyors. If mesh conveyors are used, these zones are \n   209→unnecessary unless the product width is quite wide. \n   210→A second issue is the question of independent top and \n   211→bottom heat control. A common conception in SMT \n   212→200 c \n   213→I PREHEAT1 I PREHEAT2 I REFLOW I \n   214→I \n   215→I \n   216→I \n   217→I \n   218→I \n   219→I \n   220→I \n   221→I \n   222→I \n   223→I \n   224→COOLING I \n   225→TIME \n   226→4 Section I - Technology \n   227→\n   228→=== PAGE 7 ===\n   229→WHAT IS THE PROFILE PROCESS BAND? \n   230→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather a band or \n   231→process window.(*) The size of the band is defined by the \n   232→range of temperature deviation that can occur during \n   233→reflow and yield high quality results. This is indicated \n   234→by the crosshatched area shown in the figure. In the \n   235→example, the profile band size is indicated as 25°C. \n   236→The actual size of the band will vary depending upon \n   237→solder paste, component type, and circuit board material. \n   238→SOLDER SPATTER \n   239→AND SOLDER BALLS \n   240→CAUSED BY INSUF- \n   241→FICIENT DRYING. BURNT BOARDS, \n   242→DEWET SOLDER fi COLD SOLDER \n   243→JOINTS, DULL \n   244→SOLDER JOINTS \n   245→(TOO COLD) \n   246→REFLOW SOLDERING DEFECTS \n   247→If the product temperature profile is not maintained \n   248→within the band, a defect will occur. Chip capacitors \n   249→may crack if the rise rate in the preheat zone is exces- \n   250→sive. Solder spatter and solder balls may occur if drying \n   251→is excessive or insufficient. If drying is excessive, the \n   252→solder paste may skin over and not allow any of the \n   253→volatiles to escape. As the temperature of the solder joint \n   254→continues to heat, the vapor pressure increases until the \n   255→solder joint virtually explodes as the gases escape rap- \n   256→idly. The result is solder spatter and solder balls. If the \n   257→preheat is insufficient, solder spatter and balls may occur \n   258→in the reflow zone when higher heating rates occur. It is \n   259→critical that the paste is dried at low controlled heating \n   260→rates. In the reflow zone, insufficient heat will result in \n   261→non-reflowed or partially reflowed solder joints. The joints will have a dull, rough surface finish in this case. \n   262→Excessive heating in the reflow zone may result in burnt \n   263→boards, damaged components, or possibly solder \n   264→dewetting. In a dewet solder joint, the solder is reflowed \n   265→but it is pulled away from the pad or the component lead. \n   266→DEFINE THE BAND \n   267→The profile band needs to be defined for the given SMT \n   268→assembly. To define this band, one should be aware of \n   269→not only the solder paste requirements, but also any \n   270→specific requirements of components or the circuit board \n   271→material. The process band width is defined as the total \n   272→deviation in temperature that can occur and yield reliable \n   273→results. \n   274→OVEN PERFORMANCE \n   275→From a process standpoint, the reflow oven must be able \n   276→to produce consistent results within the reflow profile \n   277→band. The user should be aware of all variables that \n   278→influence the band. These variables may be defined as \n   279→product related and oven related. By far the most critical \n   280→product related non-uniformity is that created by large \n   281→mass differences on the product. Since it is easier to heat \n   282→an area that has no components as compared with an area \n   283→with large components, temperature differentials will \n   284→exist on the product. Machine related non-uniformities \n   285→such as repeatability under product load and edge-to- \n   286→center and front-to-back product heating uniformities \n   287→must also be considered. To achieve high quality, low \n   288→defect soldering results, the sum of all the non-uniformi- \n   289→ties must fall within the defined reflow profile band. \n   290→In reality the profile is not a line, but rather \n   291→a range of temperatures all solder joints \n   292→must be exposed. \n   293→The total temperature deviation from \n   294→component thermal mismatch, oven load- \n   295→ing, and oven uniformity must be within \n   296→the band. \n   297→Section I - Technology 5 \n   298→\n   299→=== PAGE 8 ===\n   300→WHAT ARE THE CAUSES OF NON-UNIFORM HEATING? \n   301→In order to fully comprehend the capability to heat within \n   302→the defined process band, one needs to better understand \n   303→the causes of heating non-uniformities. The three main \n   304→causes of non-uniform heating in the SMT reflow pro- \n   305→cess include mass differential on the product, conveyor \n   306→and heater edge effects, and product loading. \n   307→MASS DIFFERENTIAL \n   308→The amount of temperature rise of a product as it travels \n   309→through a heating zone depends upon a number of \n   310→variables. The temperature rise of an object subjected to \n   311→heat is determined by the following equation: \n   312→AT = Q x AX t / (M x Cp) \n   313→WHERE: \n   314→AT = Product Temperature Rise (“C) \n   315→Q = Heat Absorbed (w/cmz) \n   316→A = Exposed Area (cm2) \n   317→t = Heating Dwell Time (seconds) \n   318→M = Mass of Object (kg) \n   319→Cp = Specific Heat (w-sec/kg-”C) \n   320→The mass (M) times the specific heat (Cp) is often referred \n   321→to as thermal mass. Greater thermal mass requires more \n   322→heat to achieve a given temperature rise. In the figure \n   323→shown below, the area populated with the PLCC’s has \n   324→considerably more thermal mass per unit area than the \n   325→area with the discrete components. This area will be more \n   326→difficult to heat. The non-uniformity caused by thermal \n   327→mass differential may be measured by thermocoupling a \n   328→lead on the largest and smallest device, and running a \n   329→profile. \n   330→HIGH MASS DIFFERENTIAL J \n   331→mmmm \n   332→mmmm \n   333→mmmm \n   334→mmmm \n   335→mmmm \n   336→mmmm OVEN UNIFORMITY \n   337→A second main cause of non-uniform product heating is \n   338→the oven heating pattern. This can be caused by edge \n   339→effects on the heaters (less heat at the end) or by heat \n   340→sinking of the conveyor system. The edge effect may be \n   341→caused by insufficient airflow around the edges of the \n   342→machine, or by non-uniformities that are inherent in the \n   343→heaters. The heat sinking of the conveyor is really amass \n   344→effect, similar to the one described above. The oven \n   345→uniformity may be measured by thermocoupling a bare \n   346→board over a matrix of points, and running a profile. The \n   347→matrix should include points on the board edges (front, \n   348→back, left, and right), and the center of the board. \n   349→OVEN REPEATABILITY \n   350→Repeatability refers to the oven’s capability to repeat a \n   351→given profile. Repeatability is affected by machine \n   352→loading. The figure below shows a typical product \n   353→loading condition. The “Loading Factor” is defined in \n   354→the figure. \n   355→PRODUCT FLOW INTO OVEN b \n   356→L \n   357→LOADING FACTOR = L+s \n   358→The higher the value of the loading factor, the more \n   359→difficult it is for any oven to give repeatable results. \n   360→Ovens will generally specify a maximum loading factor \n   361→(ranging from 0.5 to 0.9). The loading factor becomes an \n   362→important consideration when sizing an oven. The non- \n   363→uniformity caused by repeatability can be measured by \n   364→running a profile on a test board, then load the oven \n   365→down. Periodically, run the test board within the oven \n   366→load and compare with the unloaded profile. \n   367→The three main causes of non-uniform heating in \n   368→the SMT reflow process include mass differential \n   369→on the product, conveyor and heater edge effects, \n   370→and product loading. \n   371→6 Section I - Technology \n   372→\n   373→=== PAGE 9 ===\n   374→WHAT ARE THE MODES OF HEATING? \n   375→There are three different heating modes involved wit \n   376→most SMT reflow processes; convection, conductior \n   377→and infrared radiation (IR). All three of these heatin \n   378→modes occur naturally in our daily lives. Perhaps th \n   379→easiest way to understand each of these heating mode \n   380→is through example. \n   381→CONVECTION \n   382→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid (such a \n   383→air, nitrogen, or water) passes over a solid (such as a \n   384→SMT assembly). A cool breeze on a hot sunny da \n   385→provides convective cooling. Hot air from a hair drye \n   386→provides convective heating. Convection heating c \n   387→cooling requires contact of the flow with the solid par \n   388→Only the layer of the flow that is in contact with the pa \n   389→is actually transferring heat. \n   390→2ONVECTION I - \n   391→HOT . \n   392→AIR \\ \n   393→FLOW - COLD OBJECT IS - HEATED BY CONVECTION \n   394→FROM THE HOT AIR FLOW \n   395→CONDUCTION \n   396→Conduction heat transfer occurs when two solid masse \n   397→of different temperature are in contact with each other \n   398→A good example is when a pan is placed on an electril \n   399→burner. Most of the heat is transferred to the pan by thl \n   400→contact between the pan and the burner. Conduction alsc \n   401→occurs within the same mass if a temperature differen \n   402→tial exists within the mass. \n   403→Referring to a system as 100% convection or \n   404→100% IR is incorrect. A system may be dominated \n   405→by convection or IR, but the other heat transfer is \n   406→always present in signijicant quantities. CONDUCTION \n   407→HEAT FLOWS FROM HOT TO COLD TO HELP \n   408→EQUALIZE TEMPERATURES \n   409→RADIATION \n   410→Infrared Radiation (IR) occurs when two bodies of dif- \n   411→ferent temperature are in sight of each other. The best \n   412→example of IR is the heating of the earth by the sun. IR \n   413→is a non-contact heat transfer. \n   414→RADIATION I \n   415→In SMT reflow applications (with the exception of vapor \n   416→phase), convection, conduction, and IR all play a role in \n   417→the heating process. Referring to a system as 100% \n   418→convection or 100% IR is incorrect. A system may be \n   419→dominated by convection or IR, but the other heat trans- \n   420→fer is always present. IR dominant systems usually range \n   421→in the convection/IR ratio of 60/40 to 40/60. Convection \n   422→dominant systems have ratios in the range of 70/30 to \n   423→80/20. Conduction heat transfer occurs more within the \n   424→product during the heating process. Heat is transferred \n   425→through the product to help minimize hot or cold spots. \n   426→Section I - Technology 7 \n   427→\n   428→=== PAGE 10 ===\n   429→HOW DOES INFRARED HEATING WORK? \n   430→95.38 Infrared (IR) heat transfer occurs when two objects at \n   431→different temperatures are in sight of each other. The \n   432→heat is transferred by electromagnetic waves of 0.78- \n   433→1000 micron wavelengths. All objects emit some level \n   434→of infrared. The quantity of infrared energy emitted and \n   435→the wavelength of the emission are both dependent upon \n   436→the absolute temperature of the object. As the source \n   437→temperature increases, the heat transfer output increases \n   438→exponentially to the fourth power. Increasing the source \n   439→temperature results in shorter wavelengths. Decreasing \n   440→the source temperature results in longer wavelengths. In \n   441→SMT reflow the amount of heat transfer is important, so \n   442→as not to exceed the 2OC/sec rate. When operating at \n   443→these low heating rates all types of radiant emitters \n   444→(lamps, panels, tubes) operate in the medium to long \n   445→wavelength ranges. \n   446→111111lI INFRARED HEATING EQUATION \n   447→In order to understand what parameters are important in \n   448→infrared heating, one can consider the general equation \n   449→for heat transfer between the heat source and the object \n   450→being heated. Again, the purpose is not to memorize the \n   451→equation, but rather to point out the significance of what \n   452→is involved. The general equation for heat transfer is as \n   453→follows: \n   454→Q = V x Es x At x K x (Ts4 - Tt4) \n   455→WHERE: \n   456→Q = Infrared heat transfer (w/cm2) \n   457→V = Geometric view factor (0-1) \n   458→Es = Emissivity factor of the source (0-1) \n   459→At = Absorptivity factor of the target (0-1) \n   460→K = Stefan-Boltzmann Constant \n   461→Ts = Source temperature (OK) \n   462→Tt = Target temperature (OK) (5.67 x w/cm2/”K4) \n   463→Absorption characteristics of solder \n   464→paste and circuit board material makes \n   465→IR an efficient means of heating. The geometric view factor “V” is the fraction of energy \n   466→that leaves the source that hits the target. In SMTreflow, \n   467→the oven chamber designs yield very high view factors \n   468→in the range of 0.90 to 0.95. An important aspect of the \n   469→view factor comes in product design. If two very large \n   470→components are in close proximity to each other, the \n   471→view factor to a solder joint between them is decreased, \n   472→which makes it more difficult. Refer to figure. \n   473→f IRSOURCE \n   474→THIS AREA IS SHADED BY / \n   475→ADJACENT COMPONENTS \n   476→RESULTING IN A LOWER VIEW \n   477→FACTOR \n   478→The source emissivity and the target absorptivity factors \n   479→are in the range on 0.90 to 0.95 for most SMT applica- \n   480→tions. Solder paste is an excellent absorber of infrared \n   481→energy. Shiny gold components may be difficult to heat \n   482→as they tend to be reflective. Most often, however, the \n   483→board material, solder paste, and the components all \n   484→absorb quite well. The figure below shows the spectral \n   485→absorptivity for an RMA solder paste.(3) \n   486→I \n   487→loooo t \n   488→! 97.39 I4 I \n   489→96’70 96.04 I! \n   490→Control of infrared is generally done by controlling the \n   491→source temperature. Providing that the overall source \n   492→emission can be regulated, IR can provide high levels of \n   493→repeatability. \n   494→8 Section I - Technology \n   495→\n   496→=== PAGE 11 ===\n   497→HOW DOES CONVECTION HEATING WORK? \n   498→Convection heat transfer occurs when a fluid at a given \n   499→temperature contacts a solid mass at a different tempera- \n   500→ture. If the fluid is hotter than the mass, the mass will be \n   501→heated. If the fluid is cooler than the mass, the mass will \n   502→be cooled. Perhaps the easiest way to understand convec- \n   503→tion is to look at the convection equation and note what \n   504→is important and what is not important. \n   505→CONVECTION EQUATION \n   506→The following is the general equation for convection \n   507→heating or cooling: \n   508→Q=HxAT \n   509→WHERE: \n   510→Q = Heat absorbed per unit area (w/cm2) \n   511→H = Convective film coefficient \n   512→AT = Temperature difference between (W/cm2-”C) \n   513→fluid and object (“C) \n   514→The amount of heat transfer “Q’ can be modified by \n   515→either changing the convective coefficient “H’ or the \n   516→temperature difference “AT”. Increasing the source \n   517→temperature will increase the temperature difference, \n   518→and thus the amount of heat transfer “Q’ will increase \n   519→proportionally. \n   520→The convective coefficient “H’ can have a significant \n   521→impact on the heat transfer “Q”. The value of “H” is \n   522→related to a number of variables, including velocity of \n   523→the flow and angle of attack of the flow. \n   524→Increasing the flow velocity will increase the value of \n   525→“H”. For example, when one is outside in the wind, a \n   526→low velocity cool breeze on a warm day will feel quite \n   527→nice. However, a strong wind on that same day may feel \n   528→~ ~~ ~~ \n   529→Perpendicular flow convection breaks \n   530→up surface thermal boundary layers, \n   531→which enhances heat transfer. very cold. That is because the higher velocity wind has \n   532→a higher convective coefficient, and thus removes heat \n   533→more effectively from your skin. In SMT reflow appli- \n   534→cations, increasing the velocity of the flow also improves \n   535→the amount of convection heat transfer. There are prac- \n   536→tical limits to this, as too high of velocity may cause \n   537→components to shift around. The flow direction also has \n   538→a significant impact on “Q’. Parallel flow convection \n   539→is shown below: \n   540→PARALLEL FLOW WIBOUNDARY LAYER \n   541→I FLOW DIRECTION 4 - \n   542→BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   543→CAUSES STAGNANT FLOW NEAR \n   544→BOARD, WHICH REDUCES \n   545→HEAT TRANSFER \n   546→Note that in the parallel flow diagram, there is an area \n   547→referred to as the “boundary layer”. In the boundary \n   548→layer, there is no flow motion, which causes convective \n   549→heat transfer to be very low. It is beneficial to break up \n   550→the boundary layer to allow the flow to transfer heat \n   551→freely to the object. The diagram shown below shows \n   552→perpendicular flow convection: \n   553→PERPENDICULAR FLOW \n   554→FLOW DIRECTION \n   555→ttt \n   556→NO BOUNDARY \n   557→LAYER \n   558→NO BOUNDARY LAYER FORMATION \n   559→ENHANCES CONVECTIVE HEAT \n   560→TRANSFER \n   561→In the diagram showing perpendicular flow, note that \n   562→there is no boundary layer. This is the reason that \n   563→perpendicular flow yields better convective heating or \n   564→cooling results. \n   565→Section I - Technology 9 \n   566→\n   567→=== PAGE 12 ===\n   568→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF IR? ... CONVECTION? \n   569→In SMT reflow applications, there is no such thing as \n   570→pure convection or pure IR. The only way to achieve a \n   571→100% convection system would be to have gas flowing \n   572→over the product with no chamber walls. Of course this \n   573→in not practical. To have a 100% IR system, the chamber \n   574→would need to be operated in a vacuum. Again, this is not \n   575→the case. However, some systems are more dominated by \n   576→convection, while others utilize IR as the primary heat \n   577→source. For the sake of simplicity this text will refer to a \n   578→convection dominated machine as a convection ma- \n   579→chine, and an IR dominate machine as an IR machine. \n   580→CONVECTION \n   581→Both convection and IR have their unique advantages \n   582→and limitations. In convective systems, typically the \n   583→temperature of the flow is close to the desired tempera- \n   584→ture of the product in a given zone. This has the \n   585→advantage in that the product temperature will never \n   586→exceed the gas flow temperature. This is useful when \n   587→heating a product that has a large difference in the mass \n   588→of the components and distribution. In this case, the \n   589→larger components will continue to heat, while the smaller \n   590→components will not overheat. Convection heat transfer \n   591→is not thermally efficient. Air (or nitrogen) has a very low \n   592→heat capacity, which means that high volumes of flow \n   593→may be required to transfer the heat. One way to improve \n   594→efficiency and reduce the amount of process gas required \n   595→CONVECTION \n   596→Less sensitive to shading \n   597→Heats more uniformly \n   598→AT (Source-target) is small \n   599→Low thermal mass of air requires \n   600→high flow rates to transfer heat \n   601→Inefficient (20 to 30%) \n   602→Will heat leads first, increasing \n   603→chance of solder wicking. is through the use of recirculation. In a recirculation \n   604→loop, the process gas from a zone is evacuated, then \n   605→reheated and re-input into the heating chamber. The \n   606→limitation to recirculating is that high nitrogen purity \n   607→levels are not typically attainable. There is also a concern \n   608→that if the gas is recirculated excessively, it may affect \n   609→soldering quality, as the atmosphere surrounding the \n   610→product becomes contaminated with process by-prod- \n   611→ucts. Convection increases the chance of solder wicking, \n   612→as typically the lead is heated faster than the circuit board. \n   613→IR \n   614→IR systems have the distinct advantage of high heating \n   615→efficiencies and low process gas flows required to reflow \n   616→solder. IR systems are normally very repeatable, and \n   617→have the capability to transfer high quantities of heat as \n   618→needed. This becomes quite useful when reflowing very \n   619→dense SMT assemblies or very heavy boards. The \n   620→limitation with IR is that a high temperature differential \n   621→between the source and the product is required to transfer \n   622→the heat. Products with high component mass differen- \n   623→tial do not heat uniformly. They must be run quite slowly \n   624→to accomplish reflow. IR systems are successful at \n   625→reflowing products with component sizes up to and \n   626→including 84 pin PLCC’s. Large connectors and pin grid \n   627→arrays are quite difficult to reflow on an IR dominated \n   628→system. \n   629→IR \n   630→Control source temperature only \n   631→High efficiency (60-70%) \n   632→Easy to control rate of rise \n   633→More sensitive to shading \n   634→Less uniform high mass differences \n   635→AT (Source-target) is large \n   636→10 Section I - Technology \n   637→\n   638→=== PAGE 13 ===\n   639→WHAT ARE THE BENEFITS OF A NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE? \n   640→~ \n   641→Ease of cleaning flux \n   642→residues (RMA) Reflow soldering in a nitrogen atmosphere is becoming \n   643→a significant process consideration. The issue is really \n   644→not the ability to reflow in nitrogen, but rather the ability \n   645→to reflow in the absence of oxygen. Heating solder in \n   646→the presence of oxygen will create oxides, which are \n   647→generally non-solderable surfaces. In past days of reflow \n   648→soldering, the solder paste consisted of aggressive enough \n   649→fluxes to remove oxides during the reflow process. The \n   650→future trend is moving away from strong fluxes in the \n   651→paste. The future trends include two major process \n   652→changes that are driving the need for reflow in nitrogen \n   653→atmospheres; fine pitch soldering and no-clean fluxes. \n   654→500 ppm FINE PITCH REFLOW SOLDERING \n   655→Fine pitch soldering presents many challenges. In order \n   656→to obtain satisfactory paste print definition, the particle \n   657→sizes of tin and lead are becoming smaller. As the \n   658→particles become smaller, the ratio of surface area to \n   659→volume of the particles increases. This can be shown by: \n   660→RATIO = (n x D*) /(1/6 x n x D3) \n   661→RATIO = 6 / D \n   662→WHERE: \n   663→D = Particle Diameter \n   664→FOR THE SAME VOLUME (OR MASS) OF SOLDER, THERE IS \n   665→GREATER SURFACE AREA WITH SMALLER PARTICLES THAN \n   666→WITH LARGER PARTICLES. \n   667→Thus, as particles sizes decrease, the ratio increases. \n   668→With more available surface area in the solder paste, \n   669→there is a greater tendency towards oxidation. Keep in \n   670→mind that oxidation is a function of the metals involved, \n   671→the temperatures of the metal, the exposed surface area, \n   672→and the amount of oxygen present. The only variable \n   673→that one can control is the amount of oxygen present. \n   674→This can only be done through proper oven design. NO-CLEAN SOLDERING \n   675→The second technology change that is driving the need \n   676→towards nitrogen atmospheres is the greater usage of no- \n   677→clean pastes. The rapid trend away from solvent cleaning \n   678→is creating this and other needs. No-clean soldering \n   679→requires a tight control of the process. All of the compo- \n   680→nents and boards must be highly solderable. The reflow \n   681→process must not add any significant oxidation to the \n   682→process. The fluxes used are very weak, and do not have \n   683→aggressive oxide cleaning action. In addition, there is no \n   684→cleaning that would remove any solder balls from the \n   685→product. These solder balls eventually can create short \n   686→circuits on the product if they are not removed. Reflowing \n   687→in a high purity nitrogen environment reduces the possi- \n   688→bility of creating solder balls. \n   689→A good question to ask is “How pure a nitrogen environ- \n   690→ment is required?” A recent study (4) by Colin Lea of the \n   691→National Physics Laboratory in the United Kingdom was \n   692→conducted to determine the affect of nitrogen purity on \n   693→the process. The conclusions drawn in the study are \n   694→listed below: \n   695→DESCRIPTION I PURITY LEVEL I \n   696→Significant reduction \n   697→in board discoloration 10,000 ppm \n   698→Reduction in solder 200 ppm \n   699→balling (RMA) \n   700→Reflow of low-residue \n   701→solder paste < 100 ppm \n   702→The paper presents a number of excellent graphs that \n   703→show the affect of nitrogen purity on parameters includ- \n   704→ing flux spread, wetting force, wetting time, solder balls, \n   705→cleaning, residue, board discoloration, and micro-struc- \n   706→ture. It is recommended reading if your process may \n   707→require the use of nitrogen. You must determine the \n   708→nitrogen requirements for your process. The benefits of \n   709→using nitrogen definitely exist, however you must deter- \n   710→mine if the additional cost of nitrogen is worthwhile for \n   711→your process. \n   712→Section I - Technology 11 \n   713→\n   714→=== PAGE 14 ===\n   715→1 SECTION II - EQUIPMENT SELECTION I \n   716→WHAT ARE THE STEPS IN THE SELECTION PROCESS? \n   717→When selecting reflow equipment, it is important to take \n   718→into consideration all aspects that may affect the process \n   719→and production. Try to avoid becoming too focussed on \n   720→one issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may not \n   721→lead you to the optimum decision. The recommended \n   722→steps in the selection process are described in the follow- \n   723→ing paragraphs. \n   724→DEFINE PROCESS BAND WIDTH \n   725→The first step is to define the acceptable process profile \n   726→band width for your SMT assemblies. Try to be as \n   727→accurate as possible when defining the band. If the band \n   728→is too restrictive, some aspect of production will be \n   729→negatively affected. If the band is too loose, a defect will \n   730→show up in the process. The band width should be \n   731→defined as a range of temperatures (\"C) that the entire \n   732→profile must fall within. Refer to page 4 for a more \n   733→detailed description. \n   734→DEFINE HEATING NON-UNIFORMITIES \n   735→The next step is to define causes of heating non-unifor- \n   736→mities that may occur in the process. These non-unifor- \n   737→mities are caused by mass differential, oven uniformity, \n   738→and oven repeatability under production loading condi- \n   739→tions. A set of tests must be conducted with the desired \n   740→product on the oven in question. The sum of the non- \n   741→uniformities (\"C) must be less than the profile band width \n   742→defined in the first step. Refer to page 5 for a more \n   743→detailed description. \n   744→SIZE THE OVEN PROPERLY \n   745→The oven must be sized to handle the production rate of \n   746→the pick-and-place equipment. The calculation is shown \n   747→below: \n   748→LINE SPEED - BOARDS PER MINUTE X LENGTH PER BOARD \n   749→(MINIMUM) - LOAD FACTOR \n   750→Operating at a conveyor speed greater than the calculated \n   751→speed ensures that no product jams will occur on the \n   752→onload area of the reflow oven. \n   753→Try to avoid becoming too focussed on one \n   754→issue, such as populated uniformity, as it may \n   755→not lead you to the optimum decision. In order for the oven to be properly sized, the process \n   756→speed defined below must be greater than the minimum \n   757→calculated line speed. This will ensure that bottlenecks \n   758→will not occur at the reflow oven. The process speed \n   759→should be determined through testing or by the following \n   760→equation: \n   761→OVEN CHAMBER HEATED LENGTH PROCESS - \n   762→SPEED - PROCESS DWELL TIME \n   763→DEFINE PROCESS GAS \n   764→If a nitrogen atmosphere is required for solderability or \n   765→no-clean processing (see page lo), one needs to consider \n   766→the purity required and the gas consumption. Be careful \n   767→how the purity is measured, such that it is not measured \n   768→at the gas inlet. \n   769→REVIEW CONVEYOR DESIGN \n   770→The conveying mechanism is perhaps the most over- \n   771→looked aspect of equipment selection. One needs to \n   772→define whether a mesh conveyor or an edge type is \n   773→required. Typically edge conveyors are used for in-line \n   774→processing or if double-sided assembly is required. The \n   775→most important issue with edge conveyors is the ability \n   776→for the conveyor to remain straight to avoid dropping \n   777→product. \n   778→RELIABILITY/SERVICEABILITY \n   779→Once a machine is placed into service, it is critical that it \n   780→remains operational. Evaluate the product design from a \n   781→structural as well as a functional standpoint. Is the oven \n   782→designed to withstand the rigors of production? Review \n   783→the product manuals, drawings, and spare parts lists. \n   784→Find out where the nearest servicing center is located. Is \n   785→training available? These are the questions that must be \n   786→asked during the selection process. \n   787→INTERFACING REQUIREMENTS \n   788→Conveyor and control interfacing requirements may also \n   789→be an important selection issue. Make sure that the edge \n   790→conveyor fixed rail justification is the same as the pick- \n   791→and-place equipment. Ask questions about networking \n   792→machines to a host and product flow control schemes if \n   793→needed. \n   794→12 Section I1 - Equipment Selection \n   795→\n   796→=== PAGE 15 ===\n   797→HOW CAN YOU EVALUATE COSTS? \n   798→DESCRIPTION The selection steps listed on the previous page should \n   799→all be considered when comparing equipment. SECTION III - RESEARCH, INC. EQUIPMENT TECHNOLOGY~ \n   893→CONVECTION OVEN THERMAL CONTROL \n   894→Convection oven thermal control requires tight control \n   895→of two variables; temperature and flow. In order to \n   896→maintain constant heat flux to the product, it is critical \n   897→that both these variables are controlled. Research, Inc.’s \n   898→MICRI-FLO XG forced convection reflow system will \n   899→be used as an example. The temperature and flow control \n   900→are defined specific to this system. \n   901→CONVECTION OVEN DESCRIPTION \n   902→Temperature control of the system must consider both \n   903→the gas and the surrounding wall temperature. Remem- \n   904→ber, even in a convection dominant heating system, the \n   905→walls radiate IR energy worth about 20%-30% of the \n   906→total heat flux of the system. The figure below shows \n   907→the heater cavity flow diagram for the MICRI-FLO XG. \n   908→The reflow and dryout zones each receive input gas \n   909→(air or nitrogen) through a series of flow meters. This \n   910→controlled flow enters a diffusion plenum behind the \n   911→heating plenum to distribute the flow. The flow then \n   912→enters a second plenum, where the gas is preheated by a \n   913→series of heaters. The heaters consist of a wound nickel- \n   914→chromium wire encased in a stainless steel jacket. The \n   915→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM \n   916→AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE \n   917→II 4 Y \n   918→GAS FLOW INPUT FROM AIR OR NITROGEN SOURCE heaters are bent in a “U” shape such that easy removal \n   919→can occur from one side of the machine. The gas then \n   920→enters the process chamber through an array of holes. \n   921→The product is heated from a combination of the hot gas \n   922→and the hot chamber surroundings. A control thermo- \n   923→couple is mounted in each zone to maintain cavity \n   924→repeatability. A PID (Proportional - Integral - Deriva- \n   925→tive) control loop is used to maintain the cavity at \n   926→constant conditions. \n   927→The flow is removed from the reflow area by two exhaust \n   928→ducts located on each side of the reflow zone. This flow \n   929→is pulled from the zones using a high temperature, low \n   930→flow recirculating blower. The flow is pushed by the \n   931→blower through the preheater plenum arrangement. The \n   932→temperature control is accomplished in the same way as \n   933→the dryout and reflow zone. \n   934→The flow that heats the product (in all zones) is forced \n   935→through the walls in an array of holes to break up the \n   936→boundary layers on the product. The perpendicular flow \n   937→arrangement shown on page 8 is used to optimize heat \n   938→transfer. \n   939→Convection oven thermal control requires tight \n   940→control of two variables; temperature andflow. \n   941→In order to maintain constant heat flux to the \n   942→product, it is critical that both these variables \n   943→are controlled. \n   944→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 15 \n   945→\n   946→=== PAGE 18 ===\n   947→CONVECTION OVEN WITH \n   948→Convection ovens are a greater challenge to inert for \n   949→several reasons. First, the amount of flow that would be \n   950→required to transfer 80% of the energy by convection is \n   951→extremely high, and cost prohibitive. In order to mini- \n   952→mize this, recirculation ducts are added, which result in \n   953→the degradation of the purity of the source flow entering \n   954→a given zone. Higher velocities and turbulent flow are \n   955→also required, which can result in a greater opportunity \n   956→for oxygen to enter the system. Third, the pressure in the \n   957→heater cavity is positive in some areas, and negative in \n   958→others. Balancing this flow becomes adifficult task. The \n   959→job of inerting a convection oven is not impossible. \n   960→However, it should be noted that purity levels and flow \n   961→rates are less optimum than with an IR based machine. \n   962→CHAMBER DESCRIPTION \n   963→Many of the same principles apply for inerting when \n   964→comparing a convection oven to an IR oven. The MZCRZ- \n   965→FLO XG convection oven shown below utilizes a very \n   966→similar scheme to the 4470XN IR oven. The gas is \n   967→brought into each zone very uniformly using the propri- VITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n   968→etary gas plenum distribution system. The pressure in the \n   969→entry and exit purge sections are maintained at sufficient \n   970→levels to minimize oxygen infiltration. Flow meter \n   971→controlled venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow \n   972→in the system and to remove process volatiles. \n   973→In order to maintain the best purity levels in the zones \n   974→where the product temperature is greater than 150°C, \n   975→pure nitrogen is delivered to the reflow and dryout \n   976→zone. This is a key point, as this ensures that the product \n   977→experiences a nearly pure environment at its most criti- \n   978→cal stages (dryout and reflow). The recirculated gas \n   979→from these zones is reheated and used in the preheat zone. \n   980→Here, purity is not as critical, so the use of recirculated \n   981→gas is not detrimental to the process. \n   982→Purity levels in the dryout and reflow zone can be \n   983→maintained at 35 PPM over incoming purity levels at \n   984→flow rates of 1800 cubic feet per hour (5 1 cubic meters \n   985→per hour). \n   986→GAS FLOW \n   987→INPUT FROM \n   988→N2 OR AIR \n   989→SOURCE \n   990→GAS FLOW \n   991→INPUT FROM \n   992→N2 OR AIR \n   993→SOURCE \n   994→16 Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology \n   995→\n   996→=== PAGE 19 ===\n   997→IR/CONVECTION OVEN CLOSED LOOP CONTROL \n   998→One of the key oven performance issues is the ability to \n   999→repeat a product temperature profile. Repeatability is \n  1000→influenced most by product loading. Heavy or non- \n  1001→variable loading can result in the oven incorrectly com- \n  1002→pensating, resulting in a loss of repeatability. High levels \n  1003→of repeatability can be achieved only if all the compo- \n  1004→nents of heat transfer are maintained constant. \n  1005→LAMP DEFINITION \n  1006→The closed loop heat flux control method used in a \n  1007→Research, Inc. lamp based oven will be used as an \n  1008→example of one type of control. The primary heat source \n  1009→in this system is the tungsten filament (T3) lamp. The T3 \n  1010→lamp consists of a tungsten filament encased in a sealed \n  1011→quartz envelope. The envelope is filled with an inert gas \n  1012→to keep the filament from oxidizing and failing. The \n  1013→lamps are rated for 5000 hours when operated at 100% \n  1014→capacity. In SMT applications, the lamp power never \n  1015→exceeds 20% capacity, resulting in lamp life in excess of \n  1016→20,000 hours. At typical operating setpoints of 5-15%, \n  1017→the heat transfer to the product is controlled such that \n  1018→heating rates are under 2”C/second, and the IR emission \n  1019→is in the medium to long wavelength range. \n  1020→The greatest advantage that T3 lamps have is their rapid \n  1021→response capability. Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage \n  1022→change occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature equi- \n  1023→librium. This rapid response is ideal for closed loop \n  1024→control, as energy can be replaced in the heating chamber \n  1025→instantly after its removal. \n  1026→CONTROL DEFINITION \n  1027→In order for the product profile to be repeatable, the total \n  1028→heat flux to the product must be held constant. The total \n  1029→heat flux to the product is the sum of IR from the lamp and \n  1030→wall plus the convective component. Refer to the figure. \n  1031→Within 1 to 2 seconds after a voltage change \n  1032→occurs, the lamp is at its new temperature \n  1033→equilibrium. This rapid response is ideal for \n  1034→closed loop control, as energy can be replaced \n  1035→in the heating chamber instantly after its \n  1036→removal. RAPID-RESPONSE (EXPOSED-TIP) \n  1037→THERMOCOUPLE \n  1038→PRODUCT ”‘7 \\ REFLF JON. $ RE-EMITTED IR \n  1039→VECTION 1 FLOW -- \\P \n  1040→PRODUCT \n  1041→Accurate contol is not achieved by maintaining only one \n  1042→of the components constant, but rather the sum of all the \n  1043→thermal energies involved. The lamp IR component is \n  1044→regulated by the SCRpower controlled system. The wall \n  1045→and convective components are collectively monitored \n  1046→by the bulk chamber temperature measured with a ther- \n  1047→mocouple probe. There are two reference setpoints; \n  1048→percent voltage for the lamp, and the bulk chamber \n  1049→temperature. The bulk chamber setpoint drives the \n  1050→equation. If the chamber is at the correct temperature, \n  1051→then the lamp setpoints will be at their setpoints. If the \n  1052→chamber is too hot, then the lamps will decrease in \n  1053→intensity. If the chamber is too cool, the lamps will \n  1054→operate at a level greater than their setpoint. The lamp \n  1055→power is modified only enough to compensate for the \n  1056→bulk chamber heat transfer deviation. In doing so, the \n  1057→total heat flux to the product is maintained. The correct \n  1058→compensation will occur providing the bulk chamber \n  1059→temperature is within k 25°C. Product temperature \n  1060→repeatability off 2.5”C will occur if the bulk chamber \n  1061→is within this defined band. \n  1062→The ability to operate within this wide band has two \n  1063→significant advantages. The first is that the oven may \n  1064→be loaded down to levels up to 80% load. The second is \n  1065→that profile changeover times are reduced significantly. \n  1066→Loading may begin when all oven temperatures are \n  1067→within f 25°C of setpoint. This requires far less time to \n  1068→achieve the “ready” state than if the “ready” process \n  1069→window was f 3”C, which is often the case inreflow oven \n  1070→operation. Warm-up times of less than 15 minutes occur. \n  1071→Profile changeover times are 3 to 5 minutes for profile \n  1072→increases of under 40”C, and under 10 minutes for profile \n  1073→decreases of under 40°C. Even in the worst case of going \n  1074→from a reflow profile to an adhesive cure profile, the \n  1075→changeover time is under 15 minutes. \n  1076→Section I11 - Research, Inc. Equipment Technology 17 \n  1077→\n  1078→=== PAGE 20 ===\n  1079→IR/CONVECTION OVEN WITH NITROGEN ATMOSPHERE \n  1080→PREHEAT TOP (IR1) Infrared ovens do not require high quantities of flow to \n  1081→accomplish reflow. The predominant heat transfer oc- \n  1082→curs with IR, which does not require any flow to transfer \n  1083→the heat. The flow in the oven is used to provide some of \n  1084→the benefits of convection, which includes improving \n  1085→populated board uniformity and volatile removal. The \n  1086→amount of flow in the oven is more related to the quantity \n  1087→required to accomplish a high purity nitrogen environ- \n  1088→ment, which is considerably less than in the case of a \n  1089→convection oven. \n  1090→REFLOW \n  1091→TOP (IR5) DRY TOP (IR3) PREFLOW TOP (IR4) NITROGEN OVEN DESCRIPTION \n  1092→The 4470XN from Research, Inc. is an example of an IR \n  1093→dominant oven that provides a high purity nitrogen \n  1094→environment at low flow levels. The system uses a \n  1095→proprietary gas flow distribution method through the \n  1096→chamber walls to uniformly blanket the chamber with the \n  1097→process nitrogen. A slight positive pressure inside the \n  1098→chamber helps minimize external room effects and helps \n  1099→keep oxygen out of the chamber. Entry and exit purge \n  1100→sections are present to provide a nitrogen curtain at the \n  1101→chamber entrance and exit, which also assists in keeping \n  1102→oxygen out of the chamber. Flow meter controlled \n  1103→venturi exhausts are used to balance the flow in the \n  1104→system and to remove process volatiles. \n  1105→PREHEAT BOTTOM (IR2) The gas system is non-recirculatory, in that the gas is \n  1106→used once and then expended. This has the advantage \n  1107→that process contaminants are not recirculated which \n  1108→helps maintain high nitrogen purity levels. The heat \n  1109→chamber is accessible by unfastening six clamps and \n  1110→elevating the top heater vertically through the use of a \n  1111→crank mechanism. Purity levels of 20 PPM (over in- \n  1112→coming purity level) are attainable in all heating zones. \n  1113→Flow rates are in the 1200 to 1600 cubic feet per hour \n  1114→(34 to 45 cubic meters per hour) range. Machines in the \n  1115→field are experiencing total purity levels of 10-15 PPM \n  1116→(including incoming) at flow rates of 1300 cubic feet per \n  1117→hour (37 cubic meters per hour). Equipment Technology \n  1129→\n  1130→=== PAGE 21 ===\n  1131→EDGE CONVEYOR \n  1132→Proper design of the edge conveying system is critical, \n  1133→as typically only 0.17 inches (4 mm) is in contact with \n  1134→the bottom side edges of the product. If the conveyor \n  1135→has lateral warpage of greater than this amount, the \n  1136→product will fall into the oven. Thus, edge conveyor \n  1137→straightness is a key issue in SMT processing. \n  1138→As the conveyor rails heat during the machine warm-up \n  1139→period, they will grow and tend to warp. The patented \n  1140→system from Research, Inc. shown below tensions the \n  1141→rails, which pulls the rail assembly straight. The lever \n  1142→(outlined in dark) is attached to the rail. The bottom side \n  1143→of the lever is attached to an adjustable spring mechanism \n  1144→that runs from end to end in the machine. Approximately \n  1145→1500 lbs (681 kg) of tensioning force is applied to each rail to maintain straightness. The machine frame is made \n  1146→of welded structural tubing in order to maintain its \n  1147→structural integrity. \n  1148→The use of the tensioning system allows the mass of the \n  1149→rails to be maintained at a reasonably low value, which \n  1150→minimizes the thermal affect of the rail on the product. \n  1151→As the conveyo’r rails heat during the ma- \n  1152→chine warm-up period, they will grow and \n  1153→tend to warp.
